What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main reason I’m here is that I’m just… exhausted, in a way that doesn’t seem to go away with sleep. It’s not like being a little tired after a busy week. It feels heavier than that, like even getting through a normal workday takes so much more effort than it should, and then by the time I get home, even dinner or the usual evening stuff feels like this big task. I keep thinking I should be able to handle it, because nothing is dramatically different, but I’m worn out all the time and it’s starting to feel hard to just keep up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s definitely changed. I used to really love painting — I mean, I’m not saying I was some amazing artist, but it was one of those things that made me feel like myself. I’d set up my paints after work or on the weekend, even just play around with colors, and it would usually calm me down or make me feel more engaged.\n\nAnd I’ve always loved museums too, especially just wandering through exhibits without rushing, reading the little plaques, getting absorbed in things. But lately… I don’t know. My painting supplies are just sitting there untouched. I see them and I think, “I should do that,” because normally I’d want to, but I can’t seem to make myself care enough to start. And with museums, even when there’s an exhibit I would normally be excited about, I just don’t go. It feels like too much effort, or like it won’t really reach me anyway. So yes, I’d say I’m not enjoying things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, lately. It’s not always crying or anything that obvious, but I wake up with this heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it even starts. I’ll open my eyes and there’s this sort of sinking feeling, like, “Oh… I have to do today now,” and it’s hard to shake.\n\nSometimes I can still function and get through things, so from the outside I probably don’t look dramatically sad, but internally it feels like there’s this low cloud over everything. It’s there more often than not.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel pleasure when you do things that normally would bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, even when I try to do the things that usually make me happy, it’s like the pleasure doesn’t really show up.\n\nPainting is the clearest example. Normally I love it — just mixing colors, setting everything out, getting absorbed in it. But lately my supplies are just sitting there, and I’ll look at them and think, “I should paint, that would probably be good for me,” but I can’t make myself care enough to actually start. It feels strangely empty, like the part of me that would usually want to do it just isn’t responding.\n\nAnd museums are similar. I usually really enjoy wandering through exhibits, taking my time, looking at details, reading about the pieces. But recently even if I know there’s something I’d normally be excited to see, I just don’t go. It feels like too much effort, and honestly I worry I’d get there and still feel nothing. So… no, I’m not really getting the same pleasure from those things right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Probably more than I should, honestly.\n\nI keep replaying things in my head — missed family plans, little moments where I said I’d be there or meant to be more present and then just… wasn’t. And every tense conversation with my husband seems to stick to me afterward. I’ll go over it again and again, thinking about my tone, what I should have said differently, whether I made everything harder than it needed to be.\n\nIt’s not even always about one huge thing. It’s more like this constant running list in the back of my mind of ways I’m falling short. As a wife, as a mother… even at work as a manager. I’ll think, “Everyone needs me to be steadier than this,” and then I feel guilty that I’m not. Like I’m letting people down, even when no one is directly saying that to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your movements or speech have slowed down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, especially when I’m talking. It’s not like I’m moving in slow motion all day or anything dramatic, but people at work have noticed it.\n\nIn meetings, my staff will ask me something fairly straightforward, something I normally would answer quickly, and there’s this pause before I can get the thought organized and actually say it. I can feel everyone waiting, and then I get self-conscious, which probably makes it worse. It’s like the words are there somewhere, but they take longer to come forward.\n\nA couple of times someone has sort of gently jumped in or rephrased the question, and I realize I’ve just been sitting there for a few seconds too long. That’s embarrassing, especially as a manager, because I’m used to being the person who can respond and keep things moving. Lately it feels like there’s a lag between what I’m thinking and what comes out of my mouth.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts of death or suicide recently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve been sitting there making plans or anything like that. It’s more that when you ask it that directly, I’m trying to think whether I’ve had thoughts that count as “thoughts of death,” if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I get very tired of feeling the way I feel, and I’ll have these vague thoughts like, “I wish I could just not have to deal with anything for a while,” but I don’t know that I mean death exactly. It’s more like wanting everything to pause, or wanting to disappear from responsibility for a bit. I haven’t… I haven’t been thinking through ways to hurt myself, and I don’t have some plan in my head.\n\nSo I guess the answer is no, not suicide in that sense. But I do feel worn down enough that the question makes me uncomfortable, because I can understand why you’re asking it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month, I think. Not years like this exact version of it, but this recent shift — where I went from feeling unusually energized and driven, like I could take on a lot, to then feeling persistently low and kind of stuck there — that’s been going on for roughly a month.\n\nI’m trying to be careful with the timing because it didn’t feel like one clean day where everything changed. It was more like I noticed the high-energy stretch first, and then after that I started feeling lower and lower, and it hasn’t really lifted in the way I kept expecting it to. So… about a month for this whole swing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really, and that’s been one of the stranger parts of this for me. I usually have things I’m excited to do, even if work is busy. Painting is a big one for me — I have all my supplies set up at home, and normally just seeing the brushes or the canvases makes me want to sit down for an hour. Lately they’re just… sitting there. I look at them and think, “I should paint,” but there’s no pull toward it. It feels like another task instead of something I enjoy.\n\nAnd I love museums. I’m the kind of person who can wander through an exhibit slowly and really get absorbed in it, read all the little descriptions, go back to a piece three times. But recently, even when I see something advertised that I would normally be interested in, I just can’t make myself care enough to go. I’ll think about it, maybe even look up the hours, and then I don’t follow through.\n\nSo I guess the answer is… not much. There are things I know I usually enjoy, but right now I’m not really looking forward to them the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or like you're not a good person?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t know if I’d say I feel like a “bad person” all the time, exactly, but I’ve been feeling like I’m failing at the roles I’m supposed to be good at.\n\nI keep replaying things in my head — like family plans I missed or didn’t follow through on, times I was distracted or short with my husband, tense conversations where afterward I think, “Why did I say it that way?” or “Why couldn’t I just be easier to be around?” And then it sort of expands from there. I start thinking I’m not being a good wife, or a good mother, and then even at work I start questioning whether I’m really being the manager people need me to be.\n\nIt’s not always one huge dramatic thought. It’s more like this constant background commentary that I’m letting everyone down. Even small things feel like evidence against me, if that makes sense. Like one missed plan becomes, “See, you’re unreliable,” or one argument becomes, “You’re exhausting to live with.” I know some of that may be harsher than what other people would say, but in the moment it feels very convincing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical activity level or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, though it’s less that I’m pacing around or visibly agitated and more that everything feels… slowed down.\n\nThe thing I’ve noticed most is at work. In meetings, my staff has started noticing that I’m slower to answer questions. Someone will ask me something I would normally respond to pretty quickly — even just a basic decision or clarification — and there’s this pause before I can get my thoughts organized and get the words out. It’s not that I don’t understand the question. It’s like there’s a delay between hearing it, knowing I need to respond, and actually being able to speak.\n\nA couple of people have sort of gently asked, “Are you thinking?” or they’ll wait longer than usual, and I can feel everyone looking at me. That makes it worse because then I’m aware of the pause, and I’m trying to hurry myself along internally, but I still feel like my brain and my mouth are moving through mud. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because as a manager I’m used to being able to answer clearly and keep things moving. Lately I feel like I’m lagging behind in real time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yes, a little. I wouldn’t say it’s been dramatic, and I haven’t been intentionally trying to lose weight or dieting or anything like that. But I’ve noticed I’m just not eating the way I normally do.\n\nAt work especially, lunch has become kind of… optional, I guess. I’ll bring something or think I’m going to go get something, and then I either forget about it until really late or I just pick at it and put it aside. It’s not like food sounds disgusting exactly, it’s more that I don’t have much interest in it, and making the decision to eat feels like one more thing to manage.\n\nAnd then with clothes, I’ve noticed a couple of my skirts are fitting looser around the waist. Not enough that other people would necessarily notice, but I notice it when I’m getting dressed. I’ll put one on and think, “Oh, that’s sitting differently.” It’s strange because normally I’m pretty steady with that, and again, I’m not trying to lose weight. So yes, there has been some change, just not something I was focusing on until it became noticeable in little ways.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Not so much restless or fidgety, no. I mean, I don’t think I’m bouncing my leg or pacing around more than usual.\n\nWhat I’ve noticed is almost the opposite, or at least that’s what it feels like. In meetings, my staff has noticed that I’m slower to answer questions. Like someone will ask me something, and normally I’d just respond, but lately there’s this pause where I’m trying to get my thoughts lined up and get the words moving. It’s embarrassing because I can tell they’re waiting, and then I get more aware of how long I’m taking.\n\nSo I wouldn’t describe it as feeling physically restless, exactly. It’s more like there’s a delay in me — like my brain and my mouth aren’t keeping up the way they usually do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling an internal restlessness that makes you want to move around?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of wanting to get up and move around. I don’t feel like I’m crawling out of my skin or needing to pace, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIf anything, it’s been more like I’m slowed down. I know I mentioned the meetings, but that’s where it really shows up. Someone on my team will ask me a question, and normally I’d answer right away, or at least I’d be able to think through it while I’m talking. Lately there’s this awkward pause before I can get my thoughts together and actually say something. I can feel myself searching for the words, and I know people are waiting, which makes me even more aware of how slow I seem.\n\nSo no, not fidgeting or restless exactly. More like there’s a lag in me that other people are starting to notice.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

So yes, there has been some change, just not something I was focusing on until it became noticeable in little ways.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Not so much restless or fidgety, no. I mean, I don’t think I’m bouncing my leg or pacing around more than usual.\n\nWhat I’ve noticed is almost the opposite, or at least that’s what it feels like. In meetings, my staff has noticed that I’m slower to answer questions. Like someone will ask me something, and normally I’d just respond, but lately there’s this pause where I’m trying to get my thoughts lined up and get the words moving. It’s embarrassing because I can tell they’re waiting, and then I get more aware of how long I’m taking.\n\nSo I wouldn’t describe it as feeling physically restless, exactly. It’s more like there’s a delay in me — like my brain and my mouth aren’t keeping up the way they usually do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling an internal restlessness that makes you want to move around?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of wanting to get up and move around. I don’t feel like I’m crawling out of my skin or needing to pace, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIf anything, it’s been more like I’m slowed down. I know I mentioned the meetings, but that’s where it really shows up. Someone on my team will ask me a question, and normally I’d answer right away, or at least I’d be able to think through it while I’m talking. Lately there’s this awkward pause before I can get my thoughts together and actually say something. I can feel myself searching for the words, and I know people are waiting, which makes me even more aware of how slow I seem.\n\nSo no, not fidgeting or restless exactly. More like there’s a lag in me that other people are starting to notice.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself fidgeting or having an inability to sit still?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I wouldn’t say I’m fidgeting or unable to sit still. I’m not constantly moving around or feeling like I have to get out of my chair.

It’s more the opposite, honestly. I keep noticing it in meetings, because that’s where it’s harder to hide. My staff will ask me a question, and I know the answer, or I should know how to answer, but there’s this pause before I can get my thoughts organized and actually get the words out. It’s like everything is moving a little slower than usual, and then I become aware that people are waiting for me to respond, which makes it feel even more awkward.

So no, not restlessness exactly. More like I’m delayed somehow. Like there’s a lag between thinking and speaking.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main reason I’m here is that I’m just… exhausted, in a way that doesn’t seem to go away with sleep. It’s not like being a little tired after a busy week. It feels heavier than that, like even getting through a normal workday takes so much more effort than it should, and then by the time I get home, even dinner or the usual evening stuff feels like this big task. I keep thinking I should be able to handle it, because nothing is dramatically different, but I’m worn out all the time and it’s starting to feel hard to just keep up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s definitely changed. I used to really love painting — I mean, I’m not saying I was some amazing artist, but it was one of those things that made me feel like myself. I’d set up my paints after work or on the weekend, even just play around with colors, and it would usually calm me down or make me feel more engaged.\n\nAnd I’ve always loved museums too, especially just wandering through exhibits without rushing, reading the little plaques, getting absorbed in things. But lately… I don’t know. My painting supplies are just sitting there untouched. I see them and I think, “I should do that,” because normally I’d want to, but I can’t seem to make myself care enough to start. And with museums, even when there’s an exhibit I would normally be excited about, I just don’t go. It feels like too much effort, or like it won’t really reach me anyway. So yes, I’d say I’m not enjoying things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, lately. It’s not always crying or anything that obvious, but I wake up with this heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it even starts. I’ll open my eyes and there’s this sort of sinking feeling, like, “Oh… I have to do today now,” and it’s hard to shake.\n\nSometimes I can still function and get through things, so from the outside I probably don’t look dramatically sad, but internally it feels like there’s this low cloud over everything. It’s there more often than not.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel pleasure when you do things that normally would bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, even when I try to do the things that usually make me happy, it’s like the pleasure doesn’t really show up.\n\nPainting is the clearest example. Normally I love it — just mixing colors, setting everything out, getting absorbed in it. But lately my supplies are just sitting there, and I’ll look at them and think, “I should paint, that would probably be good for me,” but I can’t make myself care enough to actually start. It feels strangely empty, like the part of me that would usually want to do it just isn’t responding.\n\nAnd museums are similar. I usually really enjoy wandering through exhibits, taking my time, looking at details, reading about the pieces. But recently even if I know there’s something I’d normally be excited to see, I just don’t go. It feels like too much effort, and honestly I worry I’d get there and still feel nothing. So… no, I’m not really getting the same pleasure from those things right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Probably more than I should, honestly.\n\nI keep replaying things in my head — missed family plans, little moments where I said I’d be there or meant to be more present and then just… wasn’t. And every tense conversation with my husband seems to stick to me afterward. I’ll go over it again and again, thinking about my tone, what I should have said differently, whether I made everything harder than it needed to be.\n\nIt’s not even always about one huge thing. It’s more like this constant running list in the back of my mind of ways I’m falling short. As a wife, as a mother… even at work as a manager. I’ll think, “Everyone needs me to be steadier than this,” and then I feel guilty that I’m not. Like I’m letting people down, even when no one is directly saying that to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your movements or speech have slowed down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, especially when I’m talking. It’s not like I’m moving in slow motion all day or anything dramatic, but people at work have noticed it.\n\nIn meetings, my staff will ask me something fairly straightforward, something I normally would answer quickly, and there’s this pause before I can get the thought organized and actually say it. I can feel everyone waiting, and then I get self-conscious, which probably makes it worse. It’s like the words are there somewhere, but they take longer to come forward.\n\nA couple of times someone has sort of gently jumped in or rephrased the question, and I realize I’ve just been sitting there for a few seconds too long. That’s embarrassing, especially as a manager, because I’m used to being the person who can respond and keep things moving. Lately it feels like there’s a lag between what I’m thinking and what comes out of my mouth.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts of death or suicide recently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve been sitting there making plans or anything like that. It’s more that when you ask it that directly, I’m trying to think whether I’ve had thoughts that count as “thoughts of death,” if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I get very tired of feeling the way I feel, and I’ll have these vague thoughts like, “I wish I could just not have to deal with anything for a while,” but I don’t know that I mean death exactly. It’s more like wanting everything to pause, or wanting to disappear from responsibility for a bit. I haven’t… I haven’t been thinking through ways to hurt myself, and I don’t have some plan in my head.\n\nSo I guess the answer is no, not suicide in that sense. But I do feel worn down enough that the question makes me uncomfortable, because I can understand why you’re asking it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month, I think. Not years like this exact version of it, but this recent shift — where I went from feeling unusually energized and driven, like I could take on a lot, to then feeling persistently low and kind of stuck there — that’s been going on for roughly a month.\n\nI’m trying to be careful with the timing because it didn’t feel like one clean day where everything changed. It was more like I noticed the high-energy stretch first, and then after that I started feeling lower and lower, and it hasn’t really lifted in the way I kept expecting it to. So… about a month for this whole swing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really, and that’s been one of the stranger parts of this for me. I usually have things I’m excited to do, even if work is busy. Painting is a big one for me — I have all my supplies set up at home, and normally just seeing the brushes or the canvases makes me want to sit down for an hour. Lately they’re just… sitting there. I look at them and think, “I should paint,” but there’s no pull toward it. It feels like another task instead of something I enjoy.\n\nAnd I love museums. I’m the kind of person who can wander through an exhibit slowly and really get absorbed in it, read all the little descriptions, go back to a piece three times. But recently, even when I see something advertised that I would normally be interested in, I just can’t make myself care enough to go. I’ll think about it, maybe even look up the hours, and then I don’t follow through.\n\nSo I guess the answer is… not much. There are things I know I usually enjoy, but right now I’m not really looking forward to them the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or like you're not a good person?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t know if I’d say I feel like a “bad person” all the time, exactly, but I’ve been feeling like I’m failing at the roles I’m supposed to be good at.\n\nI keep replaying things in my head — like family plans I missed or didn’t follow through on, times I was distracted or short with my husband, tense conversations where afterward I think, “Why did I say it that way?” or “Why couldn’t I just be easier to be around?” And then it sort of expands from there. I start thinking I’m not being a good wife, or a good mother, and then even at work I start questioning whether I’m really being the manager people need me to be.\n\nIt’s not always one huge dramatic thought. It’s more like this constant background commentary that I’m letting everyone down. Even small things feel like evidence against me, if that makes sense. Like one missed plan becomes, “See, you’re unreliable,” or one argument becomes, “You’re exhausting to live with.” I know some of that may be harsher than what other people would say, but in the moment it feels very convincing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical activity level or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, though it’s less that I’m pacing around or visibly agitated and more that everything feels… slowed down.\n\nThe thing I’ve noticed most is at work. In meetings, my staff has started noticing that I’m slower to answer questions. Someone will ask me something I would normally respond to pretty quickly — even just a basic decision or clarification — and there’s this pause before I can get my thoughts organized and get the words out. It’s not that I don’t understand the question. It’s like there’s a delay between hearing it, knowing I need to respond, and actually being able to speak.\n\nA couple of people have sort of gently asked, “Are you thinking?” or they’ll wait longer than usual, and I can feel everyone looking at me. That makes it worse because then I’m aware of the pause, and I’m trying to hurry myself along internally, but I still feel like my brain and my mouth are moving through mud. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because as a manager I’m used to being able to answer clearly and keep things moving. Lately I feel like I’m lagging behind in real time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yes, a little. I wouldn’t say it’s been dramatic, and I haven’t been intentionally trying to lose weight or dieting or anything like that. But I’ve noticed I’m just not eating the way I normally do.\n\nAt work especially, lunch has become kind of… optional, I guess. I’ll bring something or think I’m going to go get something, and then I either forget about it until really late or I just pick at it and put it aside. It’s not like food sounds disgusting exactly, it’s more that I don’t have much interest in it, and making the decision to eat feels like one more thing to manage.\n\nAnd then with clothes, I’ve noticed a couple of my skirts are fitting looser around the waist. Lately it feels like there’s a lag between what I’m thinking and what comes out of my mouth.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts of death or suicide recently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve been sitting there making plans or anything like that. It’s more that when you ask it that directly, I’m trying to think whether I’ve had thoughts that count as “thoughts of death,” if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I get very tired of feeling the way I feel, and I’ll have these vague thoughts like, “I wish I could just not have to deal with anything for a while,” but I don’t know that I mean death exactly. It’s more like wanting everything to pause, or wanting to disappear from responsibility for a bit. I haven’t… I haven’t been thinking through ways to hurt myself, and I don’t have some plan in my head.\n\nSo I guess the answer is no, not suicide in that sense. But I do feel worn down enough that the question makes me uncomfortable, because I can understand why you’re asking it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month, I think. Not years like this exact version of it, but this recent shift — where I went from feeling unusually energized and driven, like I could take on a lot, to then feeling persistently low and kind of stuck there — that’s been going on for roughly a month.\n\nI’m trying to be careful with the timing because it didn’t feel like one clean day where everything changed. It was more like I noticed the high-energy stretch first, and then after that I started feeling lower and lower, and it hasn’t really lifted in the way I kept expecting it to. So… about a month for this whole swing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really, and that’s been one of the stranger parts of this for me. I usually have things I’m excited to do, even if work is busy. Painting is a big one for me — I have all my supplies set up at home, and normally just seeing the brushes or the canvases makes me want to sit down for an hour. Lately they’re just… sitting there. I look at them and think, “I should paint,” but there’s no pull toward it. It feels like another task instead of something I enjoy.\n\nAnd I love museums. I’m the kind of person who can wander through an exhibit slowly and really get absorbed in it, read all the little descriptions, go back to a piece three times. But recently, even when I see something advertised that I would normally be interested in, I just can’t make myself care enough to go. I’ll think about it, maybe even look up the hours, and then I don’t follow through.\n\nSo I guess the answer is… not much. There are things I know I usually enjoy, but right now I’m not really looking forward to them the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or like you're not a good person?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t know if I’d say I feel like a “bad person” all the time, exactly, but I’ve been feeling like I’m failing at the roles I’m supposed to be good at.\n\nI keep replaying things in my head — like family plans I missed or didn’t follow through on, times I was distracted or short with my husband, tense conversations where afterward I think, “Why did I say it that way?” or “Why couldn’t I just be easier to be around?” And then it sort of expands from there. I start thinking I’m not being a good wife, or a good mother, and then even at work I start questioning whether I’m really being the manager people need me to be.\n\nIt’s not always one huge dramatic thought. It’s more like this constant background commentary that I’m letting everyone down. Even small things feel like evidence against me, if that makes sense. Like one missed plan becomes, “See, you’re unreliable,” or one argument becomes, “You’re exhausting to live with.” I know some of that may be harsher than what other people would say, but in the moment it feels very convincing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical activity level or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, though it’s less that I’m pacing around or visibly agitated and more that everything feels… slowed down.\n\nThe thing I’ve noticed most is at work. In meetings, my staff has started noticing that I’m slower to answer questions. Someone will ask me something I would normally respond to pretty quickly — even just a basic decision or clarification — and there’s this pause before I can get my thoughts organized and get the words out. It’s not that I don’t understand the question. It’s like there’s a delay between hearing it, knowing I need to respond, and actually being able to speak.\n\nA couple of people have sort of gently asked, “Are you thinking?” or they’ll wait longer than usual, and I can feel everyone looking at me. That makes it worse because then I’m aware of the pause, and I’m trying to hurry myself along internally, but I still feel like my brain and my mouth are moving through mud. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because as a manager I’m used to being able to answer clearly and keep things moving. Lately I feel like I’m lagging behind in real time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yes, a little. I wouldn’t say it’s been dramatic, and I haven’t been intentionally trying to lose weight or dieting or anything like that. But I’ve noticed I’m just not eating the way I normally do.\n\nAt work especially, lunch has become kind of… optional, I guess. I’ll bring something or think I’m going to go get something, and then I either forget about it until really late or I just pick at it and put it aside. It’s not like food sounds disgusting exactly, it’s more that I don’t have much interest in it, and making the decision to eat feels like one more thing to manage.\n\nAnd then with clothes, I’ve noticed a couple of my skirts are fitting looser around the waist. Not enough that other people would necessarily notice, but I notice it when I’m getting dressed. I’ll put one on and think, “Oh, that’s sitting differently.” It’s strange because normally I’m pretty steady with that, and again, I’m not trying to lose weight. So yes, there has been some change, just not something I was focusing on until it became noticeable in little ways.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Not so much restless or fidgety, no. I mean, I don’t think I’m bouncing my leg or pacing around more than usual.\n\nWhat I’ve noticed is almost the opposite, or at least that’s what it feels like. In meetings, my staff has noticed that I’m slower to answer questions. Like someone will ask me something, and normally I’d just respond, but lately there’s this pause where I’m trying to get my thoughts lined up and get the words moving. It’s embarrassing because I can tell they’re waiting, and then I get more aware of how long I’m taking.\n\nSo I wouldn’t describe it as feeling physically restless, exactly. It’s more like there’s a delay in me — like my brain and my mouth aren’t keeping up the way they usually do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling an internal restlessness that makes you want to move around?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of wanting to get up and move around. I don’t feel like I’m crawling out of my skin or needing to pace, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIf anything, it’s been more like I’m slowed down. I know I mentioned the meetings, but that’s where it really shows up. Someone on my team will ask me a question, and normally I’d answer right away, or at least I’d be able to think through it while I’m talking. Lately there’s this awkward pause before I can get my thoughts together and actually say something. I can feel myself searching for the words, and I know people are waiting, which makes me even more aware of how slow I seem.\n\nSo no, not fidgeting or restless exactly. More like there’s a lag in me that other people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having an inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say I’m fidgeting or unable to sit still. I’m not constantly moving around or feeling like I have to get out of my chair.\n\nIt’s more the opposite, honestly. I keep noticing it in meetings, because that’s where it’s harder to hide. My staff will ask me a question, and I know the answer, or I should know how to answer, but there’s this pause before I can get my thoughts organized and actually get the words out. It’s like everything is moving a little slower than usual, and then I become aware that people are waiting for me to respond, which makes it feel even more awkward.\n\nSo no, not restlessness exactly. More like I’m delayed somehow. Like there’s a lag between thinking and speaking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It wasn’t like one morning I woke up and everything was different. It felt more gradual than sudden, but looking back, I’d say this whole shift has been going on for about a month now.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main reason I’m here is that I’m just… exhausted, in a way that doesn’t seem to go away with sleep. It’s not like being a little tired after a busy week. It feels heavier than that, like even getting through a normal workday takes so much more effort than it should, and then by the time I get home, even dinner or the usual evening stuff feels like this big task. I keep thinking I should be able to handle it, because nothing is dramatically different, but I’m worn out all the time and it’s starting to feel hard to just keep up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s definitely changed. I used to really love painting — I mean, I’m not saying I was some amazing artist, but it was one of those things that made me feel like myself. I’d set up my paints after work or on the weekend, even just play around with colors, and it would usually calm me down or make me feel more engaged.\n\nAnd I’ve always loved museums too, especially just wandering through exhibits without rushing, reading the little plaques, getting absorbed in things. So yes, I’d say I’m not enjoying things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, lately. It’s not always crying or anything that obvious, but I wake up with this heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it even starts. I’ll open my eyes and there’s this sort of sinking feeling, like, “Oh… I have to do today now,” and it’s hard to shake.\n\nSometimes I can still function and get through things, so from the outside I probably don’t look dramatically sad, but internally it feels like there’s this low cloud over everything. It’s there more often than not.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel pleasure when you do things that normally would bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, even when I try to do the things that usually make me happy, it’s like the pleasure doesn’t really show up.\n\nPainting is the clearest example. Normally I love it — just mixing colors, setting everything out, getting absorbed in it. But lately my supplies are just sitting there, and I’ll look at them and think, “I should paint, that would probably be good for me,” but I can’t make myself care enough to actually start. It feels strangely empty, like the part of me that would usually want to do it just isn’t responding.\n\nAnd museums are similar. I usually really enjoy wandering through exhibits, taking my time, looking at details, reading about the pieces. But recently even if I know there’s something I’d normally be excited to see, I just don’t go. It feels like too much effort, and honestly I worry I’d get there and still feel nothing. So… no, I’m not really getting the same pleasure from those things right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Probably more than I should, honestly.\n\nI keep replaying things in my head — missed family plans, little moments where I said I’d be there or meant to be more present and then just… wasn’t. And every tense conversation with my husband seems to stick to me afterward. I’ll go over it again and again, thinking about my tone, what I should have said differently, whether I made everything harder than it needed to be.\n\nIt’s not even always about one huge thing. It’s more like this constant running list in the back of my mind of ways I’m falling short. As a wife, as a mother… even at work as a manager. I’ll think, “Everyone needs me to be steadier than this,” and then I feel guilty that I’m not. Like I’m letting people down, even when no one is directly saying that to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your movements or speech have slowed down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, especially when I’m talking. It’s not like I’m moving in slow motion all day or anything dramatic, but people at work have noticed it.\n\nIn meetings, my staff will ask me something fairly straightforward, something I normally would answer quickly, and there’s this pause before I can get the thought organized and actually say it. I can feel everyone waiting, and then I get self-conscious, which probably makes it worse. It’s like the words are there somewhere, but they take longer to come forward.\n\nA couple of times someone has sort of gently jumped in or rephrased the question, and I realize I’ve just been sitting there for a few seconds too long. That’s embarrassing, especially as a manager, because I’m used to being the person who can respond and keep things moving. Lately it feels like there’s a lag between what I’m thinking and what comes out of my mouth.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts of death or suicide recently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve been sitting there making plans or anything like that. It’s more that when you ask it that directly, I’m trying to think whether I’ve had thoughts that count as “thoughts of death,” if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I get very tired of feeling the way I feel, and I’ll have these vague thoughts like, “I wish I could just not have to deal with anything for a while,” but I don’t know that I mean death exactly. It’s more like wanting everything to pause, or wanting to disappear from responsibility for a bit. I haven’t… I haven’t been thinking through ways to hurt myself, and I don’t have some plan in my head.\n\nSo I guess the answer is no, not suicide in that sense. But I do feel worn down enough that the question makes me uncomfortable, because I can understand why you’re asking it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month, I think. Not years like this exact version of it, but this recent shift — where I went from feeling unusually energized and driven, like I could take on a lot, to then feeling persistently low and kind of stuck there — that’s been going on for roughly a month.\n\nI’m trying to be careful with the timing because it didn’t feel like one clean day where everything changed. It was more like I noticed the high-energy stretch first, and then after that I started feeling lower and lower, and it hasn’t really lifted in the way I kept expecting it to. So… about a month for this whole swing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really, and that’s been one of the stranger parts of this for me. I usually have things I’m excited to do, even if work is busy. Painting is a big one for me — I have all my supplies set up at home, and normally just seeing the brushes or the canvases makes me want to sit down for an hour. Lately they’re just… sitting there. I look at them and think, “I should paint,” but there’s no pull toward it. It feels like another task instead of something I enjoy.\n\nAnd I love museums. I’m the kind of person who can wander through an exhibit slowly and really get absorbed in it, read all the little descriptions, go back to a piece three times. But recently, even when I see something advertised that I would normally be interested in, I just can’t make myself care enough to go. I’ll think about it, maybe even look up the hours, and then I don’t follow through.\n\nSo I guess the answer is… not much. There are things I know I usually enjoy, but right now I’m not really looking forward to them the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or like you're not a good person?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t know if I’d say I feel like a “bad person” all the time, exactly, but I’ve been feeling like I’m failing at the roles I’m supposed to be good at.\n\nI keep replaying things in my head — like family plans I missed or didn’t follow through on, times I was distracted or short with my husband, tense conversations where afterward I think, “Why did I say it that way?” or “Why couldn’t I just be easier to be around?” And then it sort of expands from there. I start thinking I’m not being a good wife, or a good mother, and then even at work I start questioning whether I’m really being the manager people need me to be.\n\nIt’s not always one huge dramatic thought. It’s more like this constant background commentary that I’m letting everyone down. Even small things feel like evidence against me, if that makes sense. Like one missed plan becomes, “See, you’re unreliable,” or one argument becomes, “You’re exhausting to live with.” I know some of that may be harsher than what other people would say, but in the moment it feels very convincing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical activity level or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, though it’s less that I’m pacing around or visibly agitated and more that everything feels… slowed down.\n\nThe thing I’ve noticed most is at work. In meetings, my staff has started noticing that I’m slower to answer questions. Someone will ask me something I would normally respond to pretty quickly — even just a basic decision or clarification — and there’s this pause before I can get my thoughts organized and get the words out. It’s not that I don’t understand the question. It’s like there’s a delay between hearing it, knowing I need to respond, and actually being able to speak.\n\nA couple of people have sort of gently asked, “Are you thinking?” or they’ll wait longer than usual, and I can feel everyone looking at me. That makes it worse because then I’m aware of the pause, and I’m trying to hurry myself along internally, but I still feel like my brain and my mouth are moving through mud. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because as a manager I’m used to being able to answer clearly and keep things moving. Lately I feel like I’m lagging behind in real time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yes, a little. I wouldn’t say it’s been dramatic, and I haven’t been intentionally trying to lose weight or dieting or anything like that. But I’ve noticed I’m just not eating the way I normally do.\n\nAt work especially, lunch has become kind of… optional, I guess. I’ll bring something or think I’m going to go get something, and then I either forget about it until really late or I just pick at it and put it aside. It’s not like food sounds disgusting exactly, it’s more that I don’t have much interest in it, and making the decision to eat feels like one more thing to manage.\n\nAnd then with clothes, I’ve noticed a couple of my skirts are fitting looser around the waist. Not enough that other people would necessarily notice, but I notice it when I’m getting dressed. I’ll put one on and think, “Oh, that’s sitting differently.” It’s strange because normally I’m pretty steady with that, and again, I’m not trying to lose weight. So yes, there has been some change, just not something I was focusing on until it became noticeable in little ways.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Not so much restless or fidgety, no. I mean, I don’t think I’m bouncing my leg or pacing around more than usual.\n\nWhat I’ve noticed is almost the opposite, or at least that’s what it feels like. In meetings, my staff has noticed that I’m slower to answer questions. Like someone will ask me something, and normally I’d just respond, but lately there’s this pause where I’m trying to get my thoughts lined up and get the words moving. It’s embarrassing because I can tell they’re waiting, and then I get more aware of how long I’m taking.\n\nSo I wouldn’t describe it as feeling physically restless, exactly. It’s more like there’s a delay in me — like my brain and my mouth aren’t keeping up the way they usually do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling an internal restlessness that makes you want to move around?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of wanting to get up and move around. I don’t feel like I’m crawling out of my skin or needing to pace, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIf anything, it’s been more like I’m slowed down. I know I mentioned the meetings, but that’s where it really shows up. Someone on my team will ask me a question, and normally I’d answer right away, or at least I’d be able to think through it while I’m talking. Lately there’s this awkward pause before I can get my thoughts together and actually say something. I can feel myself searching for the words, and I know people are waiting, which makes me even more aware of how slow I seem.\n\nSo no, not fidgeting or restless exactly. More like there’s a lag in me that other people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having an inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say I’m fidgeting or unable to sit still. I’m not constantly moving around or feeling like I have to get out of my chair.\n\nIt’s more the opposite, honestly. I keep noticing it in meetings, because that’s where it’s harder to hide. My staff will ask me a question, and I know the answer, or I should know how to answer, but there’s this pause before I can get my thoughts organized and actually get the words out. It’s like everything is moving a little slower than usual, and then I become aware that people are waiting for me to respond, which makes it feel even more awkward.\n\nSo no, not restlessness exactly. More like I’m delayed somehow. Like there’s a lag between thinking and speaking.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more specifically when you first started feeling this way, was it suddenly or did it come on gradually?\n\nPatient: It wasn’t like one morning I woke up and everything was different. It felt more gradual than sudden, but looking back, I’d say this whole shift has been going on for about a month now.\n\nAt first I think I was more unusually energized and driven — like I was pushing harder than normal and felt kind of carried by that for a bit. And then it swung the other way, into this more persistently low, slowed-down place. I didn’t really mark the exact day it changed, which is part of why it’s hard to answer neatly, but when I look back over the last few weeks, it’s been about a month of not feeling like my usual self.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really hand wringing or pacing, no. I don’t think I have a repetitive thing I do when I’m anxious or overwhelmed, at least not that I’ve noticed.

What people have noticed — and what I notice too once it’s happening — is more that I kind of slow down. Like in meetings, when my staff asks me something, there’s this pause before I answer. It’s not that I’m trying to be dramatic or that I don’t understand the question. I just need a moment to get my thoughts lined up and then get the words out. And because I’m the manager, people are looking at me waiting for a response, so the silence feels really obvious.

So I wouldn’t describe it as pacing or repetitive movements. It’s more like there’s a delay before I can respond, like my thoughts and words take longer to start moving.

I’d set up my paints after work or on the weekend, even just play around with colors, and it would usually calm me down or make me feel more engaged.\n\nAnd I’ve always loved museums too, especially just wandering through exhibits without rushing, reading the little plaques, getting absorbed in things. But lately… I don’t know. My painting supplies are just sitting there untouched. I see them and I think, “I should do that,” because normally I’d want to, but I can’t seem to make myself care enough to start. And with museums, even when there’s an exhibit I would normally be excited about, I just don’t go. It feels like too much effort, or like it won’t really reach me anyway. So yes, I’d say I’m not enjoying things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, lately. It’s not always crying or anything that obvious, but I wake up with this heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it even starts. I’ll open my eyes and there’s this sort of sinking feeling, like, “Oh… I have to do today now,” and it’s hard to shake.\n\nSometimes I can still function and get through things, so from the outside I probably don’t look dramatically sad, but internally it feels like there’s this low cloud over everything. It’s there more often than not.\n\nDoctor: Do you still feel pleasure when you do things that normally would bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, even when I try to do the things that usually make me happy, it’s like the pleasure doesn’t really show up.\n\nPainting is the clearest example. Normally I love it — just mixing colors, setting everything out, getting absorbed in it. But lately my supplies are just sitting there, and I’ll look at them and think, “I should paint, that would probably be good for me,” but I can’t make myself care enough to actually start. It feels strangely empty, like the part of me that would usually want to do it just isn’t responding.\n\nAnd museums are similar. I usually really enjoy wandering through exhibits, taking my time, looking at details, reading about the pieces. But recently even if I know there’s something I’d normally be excited to see, I just don’t go. It feels like too much effort, and honestly I worry I’d get there and still feel nothing. So… no, I’m not really getting the same pleasure from those things right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Probably more than I should, honestly.\n\nI keep replaying things in my head — missed family plans, little moments where I said I’d be there or meant to be more present and then just… wasn’t. And every tense conversation with my husband seems to stick to me afterward. I’ll go over it again and again, thinking about my tone, what I should have said differently, whether I made everything harder than it needed to be.\n\nIt’s not even always about one huge thing. It’s more like this constant running list in the back of my mind of ways I’m falling short. As a wife, as a mother… even at work as a manager. I’ll think, “Everyone needs me to be steadier than this,” and then I feel guilty that I’m not. Like I’m letting people down, even when no one is directly saying that to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your movements or speech have slowed down lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, especially when I’m talking. It’s not like I’m moving in slow motion all day or anything dramatic, but people at work have noticed it.\n\nIn meetings, my staff will ask me something fairly straightforward, something I normally would answer quickly, and there’s this pause before I can get the thought organized and actually say it. I can feel everyone waiting, and then I get self-conscious, which probably makes it worse. It’s like the words are there somewhere, but they take longer to come forward.\n\nA couple of times someone has sort of gently jumped in or rephrased the question, and I realize I’ve just been sitting there for a few seconds too long. That’s embarrassing, especially as a manager, because I’m used to being the person who can respond and keep things moving. Lately it feels like there’s a lag between what I’m thinking and what comes out of my mouth.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts of death or suicide recently?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve been sitting there making plans or anything like that. It’s more that when you ask it that directly, I’m trying to think whether I’ve had thoughts that count as “thoughts of death,” if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I get very tired of feeling the way I feel, and I’ll have these vague thoughts like, “I wish I could just not have to deal with anything for a while,” but I don’t know that I mean death exactly. It’s more like wanting everything to pause, or wanting to disappear from responsibility for a bit. I haven’t… I haven’t been thinking through ways to hurt myself, and I don’t have some plan in my head.\n\nSo I guess the answer is no, not suicide in that sense. But I do feel worn down enough that the question makes me uncomfortable, because I can understand why you’re asking it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been about a month, I think. Not years like this exact version of it, but this recent shift — where I went from feeling unusually energized and driven, like I could take on a lot, to then feeling persistently low and kind of stuck there — that’s been going on for roughly a month.\n\nI’m trying to be careful with the timing because it didn’t feel like one clean day where everything changed. It was more like I noticed the high-energy stretch first, and then after that I started feeling lower and lower, and it hasn’t really lifted in the way I kept expecting it to. So… about a month for this whole swing.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: Honestly, not really, and that’s been one of the stranger parts of this for me. I usually have things I’m excited to do, even if work is busy. Painting is a big one for me — I have all my supplies set up at home, and normally just seeing the brushes or the canvases makes me want to sit down for an hour. Lately they’re just… sitting there. I look at them and think, “I should paint,” but there’s no pull toward it. It feels like another task instead of something I enjoy.\n\nAnd I love museums. I’m the kind of person who can wander through an exhibit slowly and really get absorbed in it, read all the little descriptions, go back to a piece three times. But recently, even when I see something advertised that I would normally be interested in, I just can’t make myself care enough to go. I’ll think about it, maybe even look up the hours, and then I don’t follow through.\n\nSo I guess the answer is… not much. There are things I know I usually enjoy, but right now I’m not really looking forward to them the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or like you're not a good person?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t know if I’d say I feel like a “bad person” all the time, exactly, but I’ve been feeling like I’m failing at the roles I’m supposed to be good at.\n\nI keep replaying things in my head — like family plans I missed or didn’t follow through on, times I was distracted or short with my husband, tense conversations where afterward I think, “Why did I say it that way?” or “Why couldn’t I just be easier to be around?” And then it sort of expands from there. I start thinking I’m not being a good wife, or a good mother, and then even at work I start questioning whether I’m really being the manager people need me to be.\n\nIt’s not always one huge dramatic thought. It’s more like this constant background commentary that I’m letting everyone down. Even small things feel like evidence against me, if that makes sense. Like one missed plan becomes, “See, you’re unreliable,” or one argument becomes, “You’re exhausting to live with.” I know some of that may be harsher than what other people would say, but in the moment it feels very convincing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical activity level or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so, though it’s less that I’m pacing around or visibly agitated and more that everything feels… slowed down.\n\nThe thing I’ve noticed most is at work. In meetings, my staff has started noticing that I’m slower to answer questions. Someone will ask me something I would normally respond to pretty quickly — even just a basic decision or clarification — and there’s this pause before I can get my thoughts organized and get the words out. It’s not that I don’t understand the question. It’s like there’s a delay between hearing it, knowing I need to respond, and actually being able to speak.\n\nA couple of people have sort of gently asked, “Are you thinking?” or they’ll wait longer than usual, and I can feel everyone looking at me. That makes it worse because then I’m aware of the pause, and I’m trying to hurry myself along internally, but I still feel like my brain and my mouth are moving through mud. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because as a manager I’m used to being able to answer clearly and keep things moving. Lately I feel like I’m lagging behind in real time.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yes, a little. I wouldn’t say it’s been dramatic, and I haven’t been intentionally trying to lose weight or dieting or anything like that. But I’ve noticed I’m just not eating the way I normally do.\n\nAt work especially, lunch has become kind of… optional, I guess. I’ll bring something or think I’m going to go get something, and then I either forget about it until really late or I just pick at it and put it aside. It’s not like food sounds disgusting exactly, it’s more that I don’t have much interest in it, and making the decision to eat feels like one more thing to manage.\n\nAnd then with clothes, I’ve noticed a couple of my skirts are fitting looser around the waist. Not enough that other people would necessarily notice, but I notice it when I’m getting dressed. I’ll put one on and think, “Oh, that’s sitting differently.” It’s strange because normally I’m pretty steady with that, and again, I’m not trying to lose weight. So yes, there has been some change, just not something I was focusing on until it became noticeable in little ways.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Not so much restless or fidgety, no. I mean, I don’t think I’m bouncing my leg or pacing around more than usual.\n\nWhat I’ve noticed is almost the opposite, or at least that’s what it feels like. In meetings, my staff has noticed that I’m slower to answer questions. Like someone will ask me something, and normally I’d just respond, but lately there’s this pause where I’m trying to get my thoughts lined up and get the words moving. It’s embarrassing because I can tell they’re waiting, and then I get more aware of how long I’m taking.\n\nSo I wouldn’t describe it as feeling physically restless, exactly. It’s more like there’s a delay in me — like my brain and my mouth aren’t keeping up the way they usually do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling an internal restlessness that makes you want to move around?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of wanting to get up and move around. I don’t feel like I’m crawling out of my skin or needing to pace, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIf anything, it’s been more like I’m slowed down. I know I mentioned the meetings, but that’s where it really shows up. Someone on my team will ask me a question, and normally I’d answer right away, or at least I’d be able to think through it while I’m talking. Lately there’s this awkward pause before I can get my thoughts together and actually say something. I can feel myself searching for the words, and I know people are waiting, which makes me even more aware of how slow I seem.\n\nSo no, not fidgeting or restless exactly. More like there’s a lag in me that other people are starting to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or having an inability to sit still?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say I’m fidgeting or unable to sit still. I’m not constantly moving around or feeling like I have to get out of my chair.\n\nIt’s more the opposite, honestly. I keep noticing it in meetings, because that’s where it’s harder to hide. My staff will ask me a question, and I know the answer, or I should know how to answer, but there’s this pause before I can get my thoughts organized and actually get the words out. It’s like everything is moving a little slower than usual, and then I become aware that people are waiting for me to respond, which makes it feel even more awkward.\n\nSo no, not restlessness exactly. More like I’m delayed somehow. Like there’s a lag between thinking and speaking.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more specifically when you first started feeling this way, was it suddenly or did it come on gradually?\n\nPatient: It wasn’t like one morning I woke up and everything was different. It felt more gradual than sudden, but looking back, I’d say this whole shift has been going on for about a month now.\n\nAt first I think I was more unusually energized and driven — like I was pushing harder than normal and felt kind of carried by that for a bit. And then it swung the other way, into this more persistently low, slowed-down place. I didn’t really mark the exact day it changed, which is part of why it’s hard to answer neatly, but when I look back over the last few weeks, it’s been about a month of not feeling like my usual self.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself engaging in repetitive behaviors or movements, like hand wringing or pacing, when you're feeling anxious or overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: Not really hand wringing or pacing, no. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Definitely more than two weeks.

The clearest stretch I can remember lasted about five months. It’s hard to say it cleanly, because when I’m in it I don’t always label it right away as “I’m depressed” or anything like that. But looking back, it was months where I felt low most days, tired, and just not like myself. Things I normally enjoy felt distant, like I knew I was supposed to care about them or get something out of them, but I couldn’t really connect to that feeling.

Five months is the longest one I can clearly point to. It wasn’t just a bad week or being worn out from work — it was this drawn-out stretch where I kept thinking, “Why do I feel so unlike myself?”

    "role": "system",

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main reason I’m here is that I’m just… exhausted, in a way that doesn’t seem to go away with sleep. It’s not like being a little tired after a busy week. It feels heavier than that, like even getting through a normal workday takes so much more effort than it should, and then by the time I get home, even dinner or the usual evening stuff feels like this big task. I keep thinking I should be able to handle it, because nothing is dramatically different, but I’m worn out all the time and it’s starting to feel hard to just keep up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s definitely changed. I used to really love painting — I mean, I’m not saying I was some amazing artist, but it was one of those things that made me feel like myself. I’d set up my paints after work or on the weekend, even just play around with colors, and it would usually calm me down or make me feel more engaged.\n\nAnd I’ve always loved museums too, especially just wandering through exhibits without rushing, reading the little plaques, getting absorbed in things. But lately… I don’t know. My painting supplies are just sitting there untouched. So yes, I’d say I’m not enjoying things the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, lately. It’s not always crying or anything that obvious, but I wake up with this heavy, gray feeling, like I’m already disappointed in the day before it even starts. Normally I love it — just mixing colors, setting everything out, getting absorbed in it. But lately my supplies are just sitting there, and I’ll look at them and think, “I should paint, that would probably be good for me,” but I can’t make myself care enough to actually start. It feels strangely empty, like the part of me that would usually want to do it just isn’t responding.\n\nAnd museums are similar.
